Instanton Counting and Donaldson–Thomas Theory on Toric Calabi–Yau Four-Orbifolds
Richard J. Szabo, Michelangelo Tirelli

Abstract
We study rank r r cohomological Donaldson–Thomas theory on a toric Calabi–Yau orbifold of ℂ 4 \mathbbm{C}^{4} by a finite abelian subgroup Γ \mathsf{\Gamma} of 𝖲𝖴 ⁡ ( 4 ) \mathsf{SU}(4) , from the perspective of instanton counting in cohomological gauge theory on a noncommutative crepant resolution of the quotient singularity. We describe the moduli space of noncommutative instantons on ℂ 4 / Γ \mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma} and its generalized ADHM parametrization. Using toric localization, we compute the orbifold instanton partition function as a combinatorial series over r r -vectors of Γ \mathsf{\Gamma} -coloured solid partitions. When the Γ \mathsf{\Gamma} -action fixes an affine line in ℂ 4 \mathbbm{C}^{4} , we exhibit the dimensional reduction to rank r r Donaldson–Thomas theory on the toric Kähler three-orbifold ℂ 3 / Γ \mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathsf{\Gamma} . Based on this reduction and explicit calculations, we conjecture closed infinite product formulas, in terms of generalized MacMahon functions, for the instanton partition functions on the orbifolds ℂ 2 / ℤ n × ℂ 2 \mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\times\mathbbm{C}^{2} and ℂ 3 / ( ℤ 2 × ℤ 2 ) × ℂ \mathbbm{C}^{3}/(\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2})\times\mathbbm{C} , finding perfect agreement with new mathematical results of Cao, Kool and Monavari.



EMPG–23–01

1
Introduction

The counting of BPS states in string theory and quantum field theory often leads to deep mathematical insights into counting problems of enumerative geometry. Of particular interest are vacuum moduli spaces of D-branes and instantons, which often provide alternative contructions of the relevant moduli spaces that are otherwise technically difficult to define rigorously in geometrical approaches. Looking in the other direction, mathematical constructions of enumerative geometry can shed light on structural properties of the BPS spectrum of particles in string theory and quantum field theory.

In this paper we are concerned with the connections between BPS state counting problems and Donaldson–Thomas theory
[
1
,
2
]
. Donaldson–Thomas invariants are virtual numbers counting sheaves on a complex variety. They are defined as integrals of cohomology classes over virtual cycles of moduli spaces of sheaves. Their best understood physics connection is to type II string theory compactified on a Kähler threefold
M
, where BPS states preserve half of the
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry and correspond to bound states of D-branes. The vacuum degeneracies are computed by the Witten index and the corresponding partition function reproduces the generating function for the Donaldson–Thomas invariants of
M
(see e.g.
[
3
,
4
]
for reviews).

The enumeration of D-brane bound states on
M
can be equivalently reformulated as an instanton counting problem in a six-dimensional
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{\tiny T}}=2
cohomological gauge theory on
M
. The computation of Donaldson–Thomas partition functions from this perspective has been studied in great detail by
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
in the case where
M
is a toric Calabi–Yau threefold. For this, the moduli space of
\mathsf{U}(r)
instantons is compactified in two ways: by deforming the first order partial differential equations defining BPS states to operator algebraic equations for noncommutative instantons, and by a local
\varOmega
-deformation of
M
which preserves its
\mathsf{SU}(3)
-holonomy.

For the simplest example
M=\mathbbm{C}^{3}
, the Donaldson–Thomas partition function enumerates BPS bound states of D
p
-branes inside
r
D
(p{+}6)
-branes. A six-dimensional version of the ADHM construction in four dimensions
[
9
]
establishes that, from a geometric point of view, the compactified instanton moduli space is isomorphic to a moduli space of torsion-free sheaves on complex projective space
\mathbbm{P}^{3}
with suitable characteristic classes and framing conditions
[
6
,
7
]
. The Coulomb branch instanton partition function can be written in a simple compact form as
[
5
,
6
]

\displaystyle Z^{r}_{\mathbbm{C}^{3}}(\mathtt{q})=M\big((-1)^{r}\,\mathtt{q}\big)^{r}\ ,

(1.1)

where
M(q)=\prod_{n\geq 1}\,(1-q^{n})^{-n}
is the MacMahon function and
\mathtt{q}
is the Boltzmann weight parameter for instantons. See
[
10
,
11
]
for reviews of various physical and geometrical aspects of this correspondence.

General D
p
–D
p^{\prime}
-brane systems can be considered after turning on fluxes for the Neveu–Schwarz (NS)
B
-field, in such a way that supersymmetry is restored in the vacuum
[
12
,
13
]
. In this paper we are interested in the codimension
p^{\prime}-p=8
. Many new features appear in eight dimensions. The corresponding eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theories were constructed and studied in the late 1990s
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
. They are in many respects similar to their four-dimensional counterparts, namely Donaldson–Witten theory
[
17
]
and Vafa–Witten theory
[
18
]
on complex surfaces. The equivariant instanton partition functions on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
were recently studied by Nekrasov and Piazzalunga in
[
19
,
20
]
.

The renewed interest in these eight-dimensional quantum field theories has been sparked by recent mathematical advances in the Donaldson–Thomas theory of Calabi–Yau fourfolds, starting with the seminal work of Cao and Leung
[
21
]
which constructed a virtual fundamental class in special cases. Subsequently, virtual cycles of the Donaldson–Thomas moduli spaces for fourfolds were developed more generally by Borisov and Joyce
[
22
]
in the setting of derived differential geometry, as well as
by Oh and Thomas
[
23
]
in the setting of algebraic geometry.
The virtual cycle is defined via a suitable choice of a local orientation at each point of the moduli space. This corresponds to a choice of
signs
, a well-known phenomenon in eight dimensions that does not arise in lower dimensions. These signs are the most important and non-trivial aspects of the theory, while at the same time presenting one of the major difficulties. The computations of Cao and Kool
[
24
]
show that the choice of signs seems unique (up to overall orientation), and that they always give the simplest answer for the Donaldson–Thomas partition functions.

In the following we shall elaborate on several aspects of the construction of noncommutative instantons in eight dimensions and the evaluation of their partition functions. We give a new derivation of the equivariant instanton partition function on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
that carefully incorporates the correct sign choices. Our sign choice differs from other choices that have appeared so far in the literature.

The central achievement of this paper is a detailed systematic study and computations of the rank
r
degree zero cohomological Donaldson–Thomas invariants of the toric Calabi–Yau four-orbifolds
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
, extending the flat space treatment on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
.
These have so far received only limited attention in both the physics and mathematics literature. The analogous instanton counting problem in six dimensions has been studied in detail in
[
7
,
8
]
; see
[
4
]
for a review with comparisons to the instanton counting problems in the Donaldson–Witten and Vafa–Witten theories. In eight dimensions, only the instanton partition functions for some simple cyclic orbifolds have been briefly discussed in
[
25
,
26
]
. A thorough mathematical treatment of the rank one K-theoretic Donaldson–Thomas theory of Calabi–Yau four-orbifolds appears in parallel to our work in
[
27
]
, complementing the results of the present paper, as we discuss further below.

In this paper we construct and study rank
r
cohomological gauge theory on quotient stacks
\big[\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}\big]
, where
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is a finite abelian subgroup of
\mathsf{SL}(4,\mathbbm{C})
. This is equivalent to the gauge theory on a noncommutative crepant resolution of the quotient singularity
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
provided by the path algebra
{\sf A}
of a generalization of the McKay quiver determined by representation theory data of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, with relations given by a generalized ADHM parametrization of the orbifold noncommutative instanton equations.
The topological gauge theory localizes by construction on
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-equivariant instanton configurations. Using toric localization, the orbifold instanton partition function can be reduced to the fixed points of the moduli space under the action of the maximal torus of
\mathsf{SU}(4)
which are also
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-invariant. These are classified by
r
-vectors of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-coloured solid partitions whose Boltzmann weights depend on the representations of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
; this was also mentioned by
[
25
]
and is analogous to the combinatorial description of orbifold instantons in six dimensions in terms of plane partitions
[
7
]
. We assert that the BPS partition function on
[\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}]
conjecturally provides the corresponding orbifold Donaldson–Thomas invariants.

Noncommutative Donaldson–Thomas invariants of four-Calabi–Yau algebras already appear in the seminal work of
[
21
]
; see also
[
28
]
for the example of the local resolved conifold.
Our path algebras
{\sf A}
are always four-Calabi–Yau, and in fact they are Koszul since
\mathsf{\Gamma}\subset\mathsf{SL}(4,\mathbbm{C})
. In the three-orbifold case, this fact was used repeatedly in
[
7
]
to establish the relation between equivariant sheaves on
\mathbbm{C}^{3}
and BPS states in the noncommutative resolution chamber of resolved Calabi–Yau singularities. The wall-and-chamber structure of the Kähler moduli space is discussed in
[
28
]
for the example of the local resolved conifold, building on the standard threefold case
[
29
]
.

If there exists a toric crepant resolution
X
of the quotient singularity
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
, we can expect an analogous relation, as well as a version of the McKay correspondence which would establish an equivalence between the derived category of
{\sf A}
-modules and the derived category of
X
, when the former admits a tilting object. This should then relate the instanton partition functions in the ‘orbifold’ and ‘large radius’ phases by changes of variables and wall-crossing formulas. As such an analysis is out of reach with our current techniques, we defer it to future investigations.

Summary of Results

Before starting a systematic study of our main topic, we first review in some detail the analogous problem on the flat space
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
, in order to set the stage as the extension to orbifolds is then relatively straightforward. We elaborate on the analysis of noncommutative instantons in
\mathsf{U}(r)
cohomological gauge theory on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
with
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-holonomy and their generalized ADHM parametrization. They are realized in a dimensional reduction of
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory from ten dimensions, and also in type II string theory where the noncommutative deformation corresponds to turning on a non-zero constant background
B
-field in the flat ten-dimensional target spacetime
[
30
]
. The instanton partition function on an
\varOmega
-background is then regarded as an equivariant integral over the instanton moduli space
[
31
]
and can be evaluated using toric localization techniques as a combinatorial expansion in random solid partitions.

We evaluate the equivariant instanton partition function of the
\mathsf{U}(r)
cohomological gauge theory on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
with
r
massive fundamental matter fields. It can be expressed in an exact closed form as (Conjecture
2.105
)

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}^{r}(\mathtt{q};\vec{\epsilon},m)=M(-\mathtt{q})^{-\frac{r\,m\,\epsilon_{12}\,\epsilon_{13}\,\epsilon_{23}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}}

(1.2)

with

\displaystyle m=\frac{1}{r}\,\sum_{l=1}^{r}\,(m_{l}-a_{l})\ ,

(1.3)

where
a_{l}
and
m_{l}
are the Coulomb and mass parameters associated to the maximal tori of the global
\mathsf{U}(r)
colour and flavour symmetry groups, respectively, while
\epsilon_{a}
are coordinates on the maximal torus of
\mathsf{SU}(4)
satisfying the Calabi–Yau constraint
\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{4}=0
; we use the notation
\epsilon_{ab}=\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}
.

The formula (
1.2
) for the equivariant Donaldson–Thomas partition function on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
is well-known and has appeared many times before in the literature. We sketch a possible alternative analytic proof which, while incomplete and lacking conceptual insight, highlights the symmetries of the theory which are not evident in other approaches and can be potentially extended to theories on more complicated spaces, like some of our orbifolds. It is based on the fact that in six dimensions the Coulomb branch instanton partition function is known for generic
\varOmega
-deformation of
\mathbbm{C}^{3}

[
32
,
33
,
34
]
and is recovered from the eight-dimensional theory through the mass specialization
m_{l}=\epsilon_{4}+a_{l}
(Proposition
2.71
). The idea then is to show that (
1.2
) is the unique expression determined by the instanton deformation complex which correctly reduces in the six-dimensional limit and preserves all symmetries of the cohomological matrix model representation of the instanton partition function.

Moving on to our orbifold theories, we prove an orbifold version of Proposition
2.71
which relates the equivariant instanton partition function on an orbifold of the form
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathsf{\Gamma}\times\mathbbm{C}
, where
\mathsf{\Gamma}\subset\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbbm{C})
, to the noncommutative Donaldson–Thomas partition function for the toric Kähler orbifold
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
through mass specialisation (Proposition
3.63
). In the rank one case
r=1
, the noncommutative Donaldson–Thomas partition function on
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
is expressed by a closed formula precisely for the the Kleinian group
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{n}
in
\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbbm{C})
and the orbifold group
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
in
\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbbm{C})
at the Calabi–Yau specialization
\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}=0
on
\mathbbm{C}^{3}

[
35
,
7
]
.

For
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{n}
the infinite product formula is extended to generic triples
(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3})
in
[
36
]
. From this we assert that our proposed proof of Conjecture
2.105
should be possible to adapt to show that the equivariant instanton partition function of the cohomological
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theory with massive fundamental matter on
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{n}]\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
is given by (Conjecture
4.7
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{[\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}]\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}}(\vec{\mathtt{q}};\vec{\epsilon},m)&=M\big((-1)^{n}\,\mathtt{Q}\big)^{-n\,\frac{m\,\epsilon_{12}\,\epsilon_{13}\,\epsilon_{23}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}-\frac{n^{2}-1}{n}\,\frac{m\,\epsilon_{12}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}}}\\
&\quad\,\times\prod_{0<p\leq s<n}\,\widetilde{M}\big((-1)^{p-s+1}\,\mathtt{q}_{[p,s]},(-1)^{n}\,\mathtt{Q}\big)^{-\frac{m\,\epsilon_{12}}{\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}}\ ,\end{split}

(1.4)

where
\mathtt{Q}=\mathtt{q}_{0}\,\mathtt{q}_{1}\cdots\mathtt{q}_{n-1}
,
\mathtt{q}_{[p,s]}=\mathtt{q}_{p}\,\mathtt{q}_{p+1}\cdots\mathtt{q}_{s-1}\,\mathtt{q}_{s}
, and we weigh the fractional instanton contributions with fugacities
\mathtt{q}_{s}
indexed by the irreducible representations of
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{n}
. The infinite product
M(x,q)=\prod_{k\geq 1}\,(1-x\,q^{k})^{-k}
is the generalized MacMahon function and we have set
\widetilde{M}(x,q)=M(x,q)\,M(x^{-1},q)
.

For
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
we do not yet have available results for a generic
\varOmega
-deformation of
\mathbbm{C}^{3}
, but we provide strong evidence in favour of the closed formula of Conjecture
5.10
. It seems unlikely that there are any other orbifolds
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
for which exact infinite product expressions for the instanton partition functions are even conjecturally possible; see
[
27
]
for a geometric explanation of this.

For higher rank theories we formulate various conjectural closed formulas for orbifold instanton partition functions with particular framing decompositions
\vec{r}
of the rank
r
according to the irreducible representations of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
, for the orbifolds
\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}
and
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/(\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2})\times\mathbbm{C}
(Conjectures
4.11
,
4.21
and
5.15
). These conjectures are developed from the
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-equivariant instanton deformation complex associated to the ADHM-type parametrization of the orbifold instanton moduli space. From this we obtain a combinatorial expression for the orbifold instanton partition function as a sum over
r
-vectors of
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-coloured solid partitions, from which we deduce its symmetries and properties that are enforced in our conjectures. Then we require that they correctly reduce to the known expressions on
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
.

One of the novelties in eight dimensions is that, contrary to the standard noncommutative Donaldson–Thomas invariants
[
29
]
, our invariants depend explicitly on the framing vector
\vec{r}
, and contrary to the invariants of
[
7
,
8
]
, the
\vec{r}
dependence here is genuine and cannot simply be encoded in a multiplicative factor.
In the case of the orbifold
\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}
we obtain a conjectural formula for the equivariant instanton partition function of type
\vec{r}=(r_{0},r_{1})
for the rank
r
cohomological gauge theory with
r
massive fundamental matter fields on
[\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{2}]\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}
. It is given by (Conjecture
4.21
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{[\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{2}]\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}}^{\vec{r}}(\vec{\mathtt{q}};\vec{\epsilon},\vec{m})&=M(\mathtt{Q})^{-2\,\frac{m\,r\,\epsilon_{12}\,\epsilon_{13}\,\epsilon_{3}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}-\frac{3}{2}\,\frac{m\,r\,\epsilon_{12}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}}}\\
&\quad\,\times\widetilde{M}(-\mathtt{q}_{1},\mathtt{q}_{0}\,\mathtt{q}_{1})^{-\frac{m^{0}\,r_{0}\,\epsilon_{12}}{\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}}\ \widetilde{M}(-\mathtt{q}_{0},\mathtt{q}_{0}\,\mathtt{q}_{1})^{-\frac{m^{1}\,r_{1}\,\epsilon_{12}}{\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}}\ ,\end{split}

(1.5)

where
\vec{m}=(m^{0},m^{1})
is the
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-module decomposition of the center of mass parameter (
1.3
).

As offsprings of our conjectures, we obtain predictions for the rank
r
Donaldson–Thomas partition functions on the Kähler three-orbifolds
\mathbbm{C}^{2}/\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\times\mathbbm{C}
and
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
with generic
\mathsf{U}(3)
-holonomy, in the cases where they are not yet known. See Propositions
4.16
,
4.24
,
5.12
and
5.18
.

The
\varOmega
-deformation also enables the definition of the “pure” cohomological gauge theory on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
without the massive fundamental matter fields. The instanton partition function for the pure gauge theory is related to (
1.2
) by the infinite mass limit of Proposition
2.112
which decouples the fundamental hypermultiplet. For rank
r=1
this gives (Corollary
2.115
)

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}(\Lambda;\vec{\epsilon}\,)^{\rm pure}=\exp\Big(-\Lambda\ \frac{\epsilon_{12}\,\epsilon_{13}\,\epsilon_{23}}{\epsilon_{1}\,\epsilon_{2}\,\epsilon_{3}\,\epsilon_{4}}\,\Big)\ ,

(1.6)

where
\Lambda
is the ultraviolet scale of the quantum field theory, whereas for higher rank
r>1
the instanton contributions all vanish.

Analogously, we define the “pure” gauge theory on orbifolds. The orbifold instanton partition function for the pure gauge theory is related to the orbifold instanton partition function of the cohomological gauge theory with massive fundamental matter by the infinite mass limit of Proposition
3.99
. For rank
r>1
the orbifold instanton contributions also all vanish (Propositions
4.27
and
5.20
).

Remark 1.7
.

As the writing of this paper was nearing completion, a related work by Kimura appeared
[
26
]
, which writes down the same matrix integral expression (
3.62
) from a different perspective for the instanton partition function on the orbifold
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
, in the special case where
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\mathbbm{Z}_{n}
is a generic cyclic subgroup of
\mathsf{SU}(4)
. Kimura also gives a free field representation of the orbifold instanton partition function in that case, in the spirit of the BPS/CFT correspondence
[
37
]
, but does not discuss the explicit evaluation of the contour integrals nor the geometrical applications to Donaldson–Thomas theory.

Relation to the Work of Cao, Kool and Monavari

Our work is complementary to new independent mathematical work by Cao, Kool and Monavari
[
27
]
who consider Donaldson–Thomas invariants of general Calabi–Yau four-orbifolds using an algebro-geometric approach. In the local toric model, they compute rank one Donaldson–Thomas invariants using equivariant localization and a vertex formalism on quotient singularities
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
, where
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is a finite abelian subgroup of
\mathsf{SL}(4,\mathbbm{C})
. When
\mathsf{\Gamma}
is the Kleinian subgroup
\mathbbm{Z}_{n}\subset\mathsf{SL}(2,\mathbbm{C}
) or the subgroup
\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\subset\mathsf{SL}(3,\mathbbm{C})
, they also conjecture closed formulas for the equivariant K-theoretic partition functions, generalizing the Nekrasov–Piazzalunga partition function
[
20
]
. They recover the cohomological invariants as well as three-orbifold invariants via the dimensional reduction of
[
38
]
.

They also consider the Pandharipande–Thomas invariants of the crepant resolutions of
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
(when they exist), including the cases of non-abelian orbifold groups
\mathsf{\Gamma}
. They again conjecture closed formulas for the equivariant K-theoretic partition functions, as well as relate the Donaldson–Thomas invariants for orbifold groups
\mathbbm{Z}_{n}
and
\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
with the Pandharipande–Thomas invariants of the corresponding crepant resolutions by certain changes of variables.

Their work complements ours by developing the rank one K-theory version of the story from a rigorous purely algebro-geometric perspective, whereas we also develop the higher rank cohomological Donaldson–Thomas invariants of toric Calabi–Yau four-orbifolds from the viewpoint of quantum field theory. Up to slightly different conventions, our results perfectly match. More precisely, we can summarise the main agreements as follows:

•

Conjecture
4.7
agrees with the cohomological limit of
[
27
, Conjecture 5.13]
(see
[
27
, Corollary 6.6]
).

•

The rank one case of Proposition
4.27
agrees with
[
27
, Corollary 6.10]
.

•

Conjecture
5.10
agrees with the cohomological limit of
[
27
, Conjecture 5.14]
(see
[
27
, Corollary 6.6]
).

•

The rank one case of Proposition
5.20
agrees with
[
27
, Corollary 6.10]
.

Outline

Throughout our presentation we gloss over numerous technical subtleties without comment. The organisation of the remainder of this paper can be briefly summarised as follows:

•

In Section
2
we provide an elaborate review of the construction of an eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theory for the holonomy group
\mathsf{SU}(4)
. We study its instanton solutions, discuss properties of the instanton moduli space and define an ADHM-type parametrization. We evaluate the equivariant instanton partition function using a quiver matrix model for the ADHM data and the tangent-obstruction complex of the instanton moduli space. We discuss the relations to Donaldson–Thomas invariants of
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
and the analogous enumerative theory in six dimensions.

•

In Section
3
we study instantons on orbifolds
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
with
\mathsf{\Gamma}
a finite abelian subgroup of
\mathsf{SL}(4,\mathbbm{C})
. We extend the results of Section
2
to the cohomological gauge theories on quotient stacks
[\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}]
for generic
\mathsf{\Gamma}
-actions, providing numerous explicit examples. We also give a supplementary overview of crepant resolutions of
\mathbbm{C}^{4}/\mathsf{\Gamma}
as well as their relevance to the BPS state counting problems for D-brane bound states and instantons.

•

In the final two Sections
4
and
5
we focus on two examples of orbifolds where it appears possible to explicitly sum the instanton series, namely
\mathbbm{C}^{2}/{\mathbbm{Z}_{n}}\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}
and
\mathbbm{C}^{3}/(\mathbbm{Z}_{2}\times\mathbbm{Z}_{2})\times\mathbbm{C}
. We conjecture closed infinite product formulas for the orbifold instanton partition functions in terms of combinations of generalized MacMahon functions.

•

Appendix
A
presents some details of the generalized ADHM construction of the instanton moduli space, while in Appendix
B
we sketch possible analytic proofs of the closed expressions for the instanton partition functions on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
for general rank
r
, as well as on the orbifold
\mathbbm{C}^{2}/{\mathbbm{Z}_{n}}\times\mathbbm{C}^{2}
in the rank one case.

2
Eight-Dimensional Cohomological Gauge Theory

In this section we review and elaborate on several well-known aspects of generalized instantons and their equivariant partition functions in cohomological gauge theory on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. We do this in some detail, as the treatment is needed and is parallel to the set-up used in discussing the orbifold theories. In particular, we discuss in detail
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instantons in noncommutative gauge theory and their ADHM parametrizations, which also figure in the generalized gauge theories of
[
39
,
40
]
, where some explicit solutions are also discussed. It is instructive for the reader to keep in mind the analogous treatments in lower dimensions; see
[
41
]
for a review and comparison of instanton counting theories in four, six and eight dimensions, and
[
42
]
for the analogue discussion of ADHM-type quiver matrix models.

2.1
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-Instanton Equations

Let
M\simeq\mathbbm{R}^{8}
be an oriented eight-dimensional real vector space endowed with the standard flat Euclidean metric. Instantons on
M
are solutions to first order self-duality equations for gauge fields, which are preserved by a holonomy group
\mathsf{H}\subset\mathsf{SO}(8)

[
15
,
16
]
.
The largest possible holonomy group is
\mathsf{Spin}(7)\subset\mathsf{SO}(8)

[
43
]
, where the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-structure is specified by the closed non-degenerate Cayley four-form
\Phi
. It can be expressed in an oriented orthonormal coframe
\{e^{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{8}
of
M\simeq\mathbbm{R}^{8}
, where the metric is
\delta_{\mu\nu}\,e^{\mu}\otimes e^{\nu}
, as
[
44
]

\begin{split}\Phi&=e^{1256}+e^{1278}+e^{3456}+e^{3478}+e^{1257}-e^{1368}-e^{2456}\\
&\quad\,+e^{2468}-e^{1458}-e^{1467}-e^{2358}-e^{2367}+e^{1234}+e^{5678}\ ,\end{split}

(2.1)

where we generally use the shorthand notation
e^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n}}:=e^{\mu_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e^{\mu_{n}}
.
The four-form
\Phi
is self-dual,
\ast\,\Phi=\Phi
, where
\ast:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{k}\,\mathbbm{R}^{8}\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{8-k}\,\mathbbm{R}^{8}
is the Hodge duality operator on the space of
k
-forms associated to the metric on
M
.

The generalized instanton equations can be written as

\displaystyle\lambda\,F=\ast\,(\Phi\wedge F)\ ,

(2.2)

where
F=\mathrm{d}A+A\wedge A
is the curvature two-form of a
\mathsf{U}(r)
gauge connection
A
on
M
. The eigenvalues
\lambda=-3
and
\lambda=1
correspond respectively to the two eigenspaces of the self-adjoint operator
\ast\,(\Phi\wedge-)
in the decomposition of the
28
-dimensional space of two-forms
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\,\mathbbm{R}^{8}
into two irreducible representations
\mathbf{28}|_{\mathsf{Spin}(7)}=\mathbf{7}\oplus\mathbf{21}
of
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
. In this paper we focus on solutions of (
2.2
) with
\lambda=1
, which are called
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instantons and form the basis of Donaldson–Thomas theory in eight dimensions
[
1
,
2
]
.

The Bianchi identity
DF=\mathrm{d}F+A\wedge F=0
together with (
2.2
) for
\lambda=1
imply

\displaystyle D\ast F=D(\Phi\wedge F)=\Phi\wedge DF=0\ ,

(2.3)

where we used
\mathrm{d}\Phi=0
. Thus
every solution of the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations is a solution of the second order Yang–Mills equations. A typical solution has moduli parametrizing the center of the instanton in
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
and its scale
\ell\in\mathbbm{R}_{>0}
(see e.g.
[
45
]
). Thus the instanton moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
, i.e. the space of smooth solutions to (
2.2
) for
\lambda=1
modulo gauge transformations, is not compact because instantons can be shrunk to zero size (
\ell\rightarrow 0^{+}
) where they become singular. Deforming the equations (
2.2
) to noncommutative instanton equations provides an ultraviolet regularization which resolves these small instanton singularities.

The noncommutative deformation amounts to quantizing a constant Poisson structure on
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
defined by a bivector
\theta
of maximal rank. After using an
\mathsf{SO}(8)
rotation to transform
\theta
into its Jordan canonical form and a suitable rescaling of coordinates, it defines a complex structure on
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
. Conversely, we can choose one of the seven complex structures on
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
, consider the associated Kähler form
\omega
corresponding to the metric
\delta_{\mu\nu}\,e^{\mu}\otimes e^{\nu}
, and set
\theta=\zeta\,\omega^{-1}
, where
\zeta
is a positive real parameter. The noncommutative deformation introduces an additional length scale
\zeta
which serves as a minimum size for the instantons, so it resolves the moduli space singularity as instantons can no longer reach the singularity where their size vanishes. The introduction of a complex structure
J
breaks the holonomy group to
\mathsf{SU}(4)\simeq\mathsf{Spin}(6)\subset\mathsf{Spin}(7)
, so that the instanton equations are now defined on the Calabi–Yau fourfold
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
.

Let
\Theta^{a}=e^{2a-1}+\mathrm{i}\,e^{2a}
and
\Theta^{\bar{a}}=\bar{\Theta}^{a}
be a coframe on
M=\mathbbm{C}^{4}
which forms a basis of
(1,0)
-forms and
(0,1)
-forms with respect to
J
, that is

\displaystyle J\,\Theta^{a}=\mathrm{i}\,\Theta^{a}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad a\ \in\ \underline{\sf 4}:=\{1,2,3,4\}\ .

(2.4)

Then there is a non-degenerate
(4,0)
-form
\Omega
and a
(1,1)
-form
\omega
defined by
[
46
]

\displaystyle\Omega=\Theta^{1}\wedge\Theta^{2}\wedge\Theta^{3}\wedge\Theta^{4}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\omega=\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\big(\Theta^{1}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{1}}+\Theta^{2}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{2}}+\Theta^{3}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{3}}+\Theta^{4}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{4}}\big)\ ,

(2.5)

which obey

\displaystyle\Omega\wedge\omega=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Omega\wedge\bar{\Omega}=\tfrac{2}{3}\,\omega^{4}=16\,{\tiny\tt vol}\ .

(2.6)

The pair
(\omega,\Omega)
defines an
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-structure on
M
and there is a compatible
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-structure determined by

\displaystyle\Phi=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\omega\wedge\omega-{\rm Re}\,\Omega\ ,

(2.7)

which coincides with the Cayley four-form (
2.1
). The corresponding Kähler metric is
\omega\circ J=\delta_{a\bar{a}}\,\Theta^{a}\otimes\Theta^{\bar{a}}
.

We can write the field strength
F
in the basis of
(1,0)
-forms and
(0,1)
-forms as

\displaystyle F=F^{2,0}+F^{1,1}+F^{0,2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,F_{ab}\,\Theta^{a}\wedge\Theta^{b}+F_{a\bar{b}}\,\Theta^{a}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{b}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,F_{\bar{a}\bar{b}}\,\Theta^{\bar{a}}\wedge\Theta^{\bar{b}}\ .

(2.8)

When the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-structure is determined by an
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-structure via (
2.7
), the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations (
2.2
) can be reduced via the inclusion
\mathsf{SU}(4)\subset\mathsf{Spin}(7)
to the equations
[
1
,
2
]

\displaystyle F_{ab}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab\bar{c}\bar{d}}\,F_{\bar{c}\bar{d}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta^{a\bar{a}}\,F_{a\bar{a}}=0\ ,

(2.9)

for
a,b\in\underline{\sf 4}
, where
\varepsilon_{ab\bar{c}\bar{d}}
is the Levi–Civita tensor in four dimensions.

The
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations (
2.9
) are weaker than the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations that appear in the analogue instanton equations in four and six dimensions, which amount to replacing the first equation of (
2.9
) with
F_{ab}=0
and describe semistable holomorphic vector bundles on
M
. Any solution of the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations is automatically a solution of the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations, but not conversely in general.

2.2
Cohomological Gauge Theory

The cohomological Yang–Mills theory which describes the instanton moduli problem in eight dimensions corresponding to the
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-structure defined by (
2.5
) was constructed in
[
47
,
14
]
using the BRST formalism to gauge fix the topological action functional

\displaystyle S_{0}:=\int_{M}\,\Omega\wedge\mathrm{Tr}\big(F^{0,2}\wedge F^{0,2}\big)\ ,

(2.10)

where
\mathrm{Tr}
denotes the trace in the fundamental representation of the (complexified) gauge group. It can be equivalently obtained by dimensional reduction from ten-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory with gauge group
\mathsf{U}(r)

[
48
]
, whose field content consists of the gauge field
A
and a Majorana–Weyl spinor in the adjoint representation of
\mathsf{U}(r)
. This also identifies it as the low-energy effective field theory on D-branes in type II string theory.

One starts by compactifying the time dimension and the ninth spatial dimension of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,9}
on a square torus
\mathbbm{T}^{1,1}=\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}\,\big/\,2\pi\,R\,\mathbbm{Z}^{2}
of radius
R
. Accordingly, the field theory is defined on the spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{8}\times\mathbbm{T}^{1,1}
, and in the limit
R\rightarrow 0
the unbroken global symmetry group is

\displaystyle\mathsf{SO}(1,9)\ \supset\ \mathsf{SO}(8)\times\mathsf{SO}(1,1)\ .

(2.11)

The components of the gauge field
A_{0}
and
A_{9}
become a pair of adjoint scalar fields on
M=\mathbbm{R}^{8}
after dimensional reduction. The reduced field theory has a non-compact R-symmetry group
\mathsf{SO}(1,1)
which in the bosonic sector acts only on these scalars. The Majorana–Weyl spinor field reduces according to the decomposition of the positive chirality real spinor representation of
\mathsf{SO}(1,9)
into irreducible representations of the subgroup (
2.11
) given by

\displaystyle{{\bm{1}}6}_{+}\big|_{\mathsf{SO}(8)\times\mathsf{SO}(1,1)}={\bm{(8}}{}_{\rm s}{\bm{,+1)}}\,\oplus\,{\bm{(8}}{}_{\rm c}{\bm{,-1)}}\ ,

(2.12)

where
{{\bm{8}}}_{\rm s}
and
{{\bm{8}}}_{\rm c}
are respectively the chiral and antichiral real spinor representations of
\mathsf{SO}(8)
.

With the reduced holonomy group
\mathsf{SU}(4)\subset\mathsf{Spin}(7)
, the ambient space becomes the Calabi–Yau fourfold
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
, characterized by a complex structure
J
and a compatible
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-structure
(\omega,\Omega)
from (
2.4
)–(
2.6
). The doublet of real scalars are naturally grouped into a complex Higgs field
\varphi
.
Under
\mathsf{SU}(4)
the curvature two-form
F
decomposes as in (
2.8
).

The
\mathsf{SU}(4)
holonomy preserves two supercharges. Under
\mathsf{SU}(4)
the spinor representations
\mathbf{8}_{\rm s}
and
\mathbf{8}_{\rm c}
branch into irreducible representations as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{8}_{\rm s}\big|_{\mathsf{SU}(4)}&={\bm{1}}\oplus{\bm{6}}\oplus{\bm{1}}\simeq\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\,\oplus\,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2,0}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\,\oplus\,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{4,0}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathbf{8}_{\rm c}\big|_{\mathsf{SU}(4)}&={\bm{4}}\oplus\bar{{\bm{4}}}\simeq\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{1,0}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\,\oplus\,\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{3,0}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\ .\end{split}

(2.13)

Accordingly, in the fermionic sector the spectrum consists of a complex scalar and a
(2,0)
-form, along with
(1,0)
-forms and
(0,1)
-forms. This matches the field content of the bosonic spectrum, which consists of the complex gauge field

\displaystyle A=A^{1,0}+A^{0,1}=A_{a}\,\Theta^{a}+A_{\bar{a}}\,\Theta^{\bar{a}}

(2.14)

and Higgs field
\varphi
along with their complex conjugates. This recovers the field content of the holomorphic
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{\tiny T}}=2
topological Yang–Mills theory in eight dimensions
[
47
,
14
]
.

Here we shall focus on the purely bosonic sector of the dimensionally reduced field theory on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. For this, let
\ast:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{k,l}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{4-l,4-k}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
be the map induced by the Hodge duality operator. Together with the holomorphic four-form
\Omega
, it defines an anti-linear operator
\star_{\Omega}:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,k}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,4-k}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
by
\star_{\Omega}\,\beta:=\overline{\ast(\beta\wedge\Omega)}
for
\beta\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,k}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. With
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{gl}(r,\mathbbm{C})
, this operation has the property that the standard inner product of
\alpha,\beta\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,k}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle:=\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\alpha\wedge\ast\,\beta^{\dagger}\big)=\int_{M}\,\Omega\wedge\mathrm{Tr}\big(\alpha\wedge\star_{\Omega}\,\beta\big)\ .

(2.15)

There is a related gauge-invariant complex quadratic form defined for
\alpha\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
by

\displaystyle(\alpha,\alpha)_{\Omega}:=\int_{M}\,\Omega\wedge\mathrm{Tr}(\alpha\wedge\alpha)\ .

(2.16)

The Yang–Mills Lagrangian density on
M
can be written as
{\tiny\tt vol}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(-\frac{1}{4}\,F_{\mu\nu}^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{Tr}(F\wedge\ast F)
, and so the Yang–Mills action functional is expressed in terms of the norm induced by the inner product (
2.15
) as
\frac{1}{2}\,\|F\|^{2}
. This may be rewritten for gauge fields with suitable boundary conditions at infinity and vanishing first Chern form (i.e.
F\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{R}^{8}\otimes\mathfrak{su}(r)
) using the useful identity
[
49
]

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\|F\|^{2}=2\,\big\|F^{0,2}\big\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\|\omega\,\lrcorner\,F\|^{2}+\mathcal{K}\ ,

(2.17)

where
\omega\,\lrcorner\,F=\ast\,(\omega\wedge\ast\,F)
is the contraction of the two-form
F
with the
(1,1)
-form
\omega
, while
\mathcal{K}=6\,\int_{M}\,\omega^{2}\wedge\mathrm{Tr}(F^{2})
is a topological term.
This enables us to write the purely bosonic part of the dimensionally reduced action functional in the form

\displaystyle S=2\,\big\|F^{0,2}\big\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\|\omega\,\lrcorner\,F\|^{2}+\|D\varphi\|^{2}+V(\varphi,\bar{\varphi})+\mathcal{K}\ ,

(2.18)

where
V(\varphi,\bar{\varphi})=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{M}\,{\tiny\tt vol}\ \mathrm{Tr}\big([\varphi,\bar{\varphi}]^{2}\big)
is the Higgs potential.

The involution
\star_{\Omega}:\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
enables an orthogonal decomposition of the field strength
F^{0,2}=\bar{\partial}A^{0,1}+A^{0,1}\wedge A^{0,1}=F_{+}^{0,2}+F_{-}^{0,2}
into eigencurvatures as

\displaystyle F_{\pm}^{0,2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(F^{0,2}\pm\star_{\Omega}\,F^{0,2}\big)\qquad\mbox{with}\quad\star_{\Omega}F_{\pm}^{0,2}=\pm\,F_{\pm}^{0,2}\ .

(2.19)

This self-duality condition arises from the reality of the representation
\bf 6
in the branching of
{\bf 8}_{\rm s}
from (
2.13
), which yields a splitting

\displaystyle\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{+}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\oplus\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{-}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}

(2.20)

into
real

\pm 1
-eigenspaces
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}_{\pm}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
of
\star_{\Omega}
, since
(\star_{\Omega})^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
.

The quadratic form (
2.16
) is maximally positive/negative definite on
\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\pm}^{0,2}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
. This enables us to write the topological action functional (
2.10
) as

\displaystyle S_{0}=\big(F^{0,2},F^{0,2}\big)_{\Omega}=\big\|F_{+}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}-\big\|F_{-}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}\ .

(2.21)

From
\big\|F^{0,2}\big\|^{2}=\big\|F_{+}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}+\big\|F_{-}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}=2\,\big\|F_{-}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}+S_{0}
we can then bring (
2.18
) to the form

\displaystyle S=4\,\big\|F_{-}^{0,2}\big\|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\|\omega\,\lrcorner\,F\|^{2}+\|D\varphi\|^{2}+V(\varphi,\bar{\varphi})+2\,S_{0}+\mathcal{K}\ .

(2.22)

In particular, it follows that
S\geq 2\,S_{0}+\mathcal{K}
.

Since
\mathsf{SU}(4)\subset\mathsf{Spin}(7)
, and dimensional reduction of ten-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory on a manifold of
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-holonomy is equivalent to a topological twist of the resulting eight-dimensional supersymmetric gauge theory
[
14
]
, this field theory is cohomological and has a BRST symmetry. Hence in the topological sector with
2\,S_{0}+\mathcal{K}
fixed, it localizes onto the moduli space of solutions of the BRST fixed point equations given by

\displaystyle F_{-}^{0,2}=0\ ,\quad\omega\,\lrcorner\,F=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad D\varphi=0\ .

(2.23)

The first equation implies
F_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab\bar{c}\bar{d}}\,F_{\bar{c}\bar{{d}}}
, while the second equation gives
\delta^{a\bar{a}}\,F_{a\bar{a}}=0
.
In other words, the gauge theory localizes on the instanton equations (
2.9
) on the Calabi–Yau fourfold
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. The solutions of these equations minimize the action functional (
2.22
). When
S_{0}=0
, the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations are equivalent to the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations.

2.3
Equivariant Gauge Theory

We shall now describe the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
of solutions to the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations (
2.9
), and use this to compute the instanton partition function of the eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theory. We begin with an informal discussion of ‘integration’ over instanton moduli spaces, and then quantify how to compute these formal expressions practically in the remainder of this section through a generalized ADHM parametrization of
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
.

Generally, the BRST symmetry of a rank
r
cohomological gauge theory localizes its path integral (in a fixed topological sector) to an integral over a virtual fundamental class
[\mathfrak{M}_{r}]^{\rm vir}
, which is represented by a coarse moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
and a vector bundle
{\sf Ob}_{r}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r}
called the obstruction bundle, whose fibres are spanned by the antighost zero modes; it is non-trivial when there is a quadratic Kuranishi map obstructing the extension of first order deformations, parametrized by the fibres of the tangent bundle
T\mathfrak{M}_{r}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r}
, to second order. If
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
is smooth and oriented (which is seldom the case), then
[\mathfrak{M}_{r}]^{\rm vir}
is the Poincaré dual of the Euler class
e({\sf Ob}_{r})
. Partition functions of the topological field theory are then defined geometrically by integrating the Euler class
e(\mathscr{K})
of a ‘matter bundle’
\mathscr{K}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r}
whose rank equals the virtual dimension of the moduli space, i.e. the rank of the virtual tangent bundle
T^{\rm vir}\mathfrak{M}_{r}=T\mathfrak{M}_{r}\ominus{\sf Ob}_{r}
.

In our eight-dimensional theory, we let
P_{\Omega}^{-}=\frac{1}{2}\,(\mathbbm{1}-\star_{\Omega}):\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{-}^{0,2}\,\mathbbm{C}^{4}
be the projection to the
-1
-eigenspace of the involution
\star_{\Omega}
induced by the Kähler metric and the holomorphic
(4,0)
-form
\Omega
on the Calabi–Yau fourfold
M=\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. The local geometry of the instanton moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
is captured by the instanton deformation complex
[
14
]

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\ \bar{\partial}_{A}\ }\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{0,1}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\ \bar{\partial}_{A}^{-}\ }\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{-}^{0,2}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow 0\ ,

(2.24)

where the first arrow is an infinitesimal complex gauge transformation, while the second arrow with
\bar{\partial}_{A}^{-}:=P_{\Omega}^{-}\circ\bar{\partial}_{A}
is the linearization of the first equation in (
2.23
). Associated to this complex is a local cyclic
L_{\infty}
-algebra which describes the full cohomological field theory in the BV formalism and
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
as the corresponding Maurer–Cartan moduli space.

The cochain complex (
2.24
) is elliptic and its degree one cohomology represents the (complex) tangent space to the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
at a fixed holomorphic self-dual connection
A
. We assume that the degree zero cohomology vanishes, i.e.
\ker\big(\bar{\partial}_{A}\big)=0
, which amounts to restricting to irreducible connections with only trivial automorphisms. There is also a non-trivial degree two cohomology which defines the real self-dual obstruction bundle
{\sf Ob}_{r}^{-}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r}
, whose fibre over
A
is given by
{\rm coker}\big(\bar{\partial}_{A}^{-}\big)\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{-}^{0,2}\,T^{*}\mathbbm{C}^{4}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
.

This is the starting point for the construction of a corresponding (real) virtual fundamental class
[\mathfrak{M}_{r}]^{\rm vir}
in
[
21
,
50
,
22
,
51
,
52
,
23
,
53
]
. We consider the stratification of the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r}
into its connected components
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
which are labelled by the instanton number (fourth Chern class)

\displaystyle k=\frac{1}{384\pi^{4}}\,\int_{M}\,\mathrm{Tr}(F\wedge F\wedge F\wedge F)\ .

(2.25)

The obstruction bundle correspondingly restricts to real vector bundles
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
, which are orientable. The real virtual dimension is
2\,r\,k
and the virtual fundamental class
[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}
depends on the choice of an orientation
\mathtt{o}
of
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}
.

The Euler class of the self-dual obstruction bundle, which is naturally induced by integration over the BRST antighost fields in the cohomological gauge theory, can be thought of as defining a square root Euler class for the complexification
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}:={\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}\mathbbm{C}
through

\displaystyle\sqrt{e}({\sf Ob}_{r,k}):=e({\sf Ob}^{-}_{r,k})\ .

(2.26)

In
[
23
]
an alternative definition of the square root Euler class is given as follows. The complex vector bundle
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}
carries a natural quadratic form induced by (
2.16
), which restricts to a metric on
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}
. Then
\sqrt{e}({\sf Ob}_{r,k})
can be defined using a maximally isotropic holomorphic sub-bundle of
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}
with respect to this quadratic form, which is isomorphic to
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}
as a real bundle. This comes with a sign determined by the choice of orientation. It has the advantage of yielding a class that lifts to Chow cohomology (with
\mathbbm{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
-coefficients) and provides a construction of
[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}
as an algebraic cycle.

A natural choice of ‘matter bundle’
\mathscr{K}
in this case is provided by the bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
whose fibre over a gauge orbit
[A]
is the complex vector space
V
entering the generalized ADHM parametrization of
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
in Section
2.4
below; this is a complex vector bundle of rank
k
, and taking
r
tensor powers of it gives a vector bundle
\mathscr{K}
of the desired real rank
2\,r\,k
. To incorporate masses
\vec{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})
for the matter fields we use the Chern polynomial of the vector bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}
which is defined by the formula

\displaystyle c(\mathscr{V}_{r,k};m)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\,m^{i}\,c_{k-i}(\mathscr{V}_{r,k})\ ,

(2.27)

where
c_{i}(\mathscr{V}_{r,k})
is the
i
-th Chern class of
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}
. The Euler class is the top Chern class
e(\mathscr{V}_{r,k})=c_{k}(\mathscr{V}_{r,k})
.

The instanton partition function of the eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theory then schematically has the form

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}^{r}(\mathtt{q};\vec{m})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\mathtt{q}^{k}\ \int_{[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}}\ \prod_{l=1}^{r}\,c(\mathscr{V}_{r,k};m_{l})\ ,

(2.28)

where the counting parameter
\mathtt{q}
weighs the instanton number and is determined by the couplings of the gauge theory.

To make sense of the symbolic expression (
2.28
), we will work in the setting of equivariant gauge theory and
define
it via an equivariant integral over the instanton moduli space. The global symmetry group of the cohomological field theory with matter is

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}\,\times\,\mathsf{SU}(4)\,\times\,\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}\ ,

(2.29)

where the colour symmetry
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}
acts by rotating the framing of the gauge bundle at infinity in
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
, and the flavour symmetry
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}
acts on its vector representation
{\bm{r}}
. This group can be rotated into its maximal torus

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a}}\,\times\,\mathsf{T}_{\vec{\epsilon}}\,\times\,\mathsf{T}_{\vec{m}}\ ,

(2.30)

where we label the torus factors by the corresponding complexified Cartan subalgebra elements, which we assume to consist of generic complex parameters in the following.

The framing rotation parameters
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
are vacuum expectation values of the complex Higgs field
\varphi
, while
\vec{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4})
satisfy

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}=0

(2.31)

and correspond to the natural complex scaling action of the three-torus
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
. This couples the gauge theory to Nekrasov’s
\varOmega
-background
[
31
]
through an
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-invariant deformation of the BRST supercharges. The
\varOmega
-deformation does not change the instanton equations (
2.9
), but it confines their solutions to the fixed point
0\in\mathbbm{C}^{4}
and so provides an infrared regularization of the singularities of
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
due to instantons that escape to infinity.
The flavour rotation parameters
\vec{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})
are masses of
r
fundamental matter fields.

The torus
\mathsf{T}
acts on the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
and its obstruction bundle
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}
(with
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{m}}
acting trivially), as well as on the vector bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\otimes{\bm{r}}
(with
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{m}}
acting trivially on
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}
and
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon}}:=\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a}}\times\mathsf{T}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
acting trivially on
{\bm{r}}
).
The product of Chern polynomials in (
2.28
) can be regarded as the equivariant Euler class
e_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\otimes{\bm{r}})
. The integrations are then interpreted as the pushforwards
\int_{[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}}^{\mathsf{T}}\,e_{\mathsf{T}}(\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\otimes{\bm{r}})
to a point in the
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant Chow cohomology of
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
, whose coefficient ring is
\mathbbm{C}[\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon},\vec{m}]/\langle\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{4}\rangle
. The
\varOmega
-deformation localizes the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
onto its isolated
\mathsf{T}
-fixed points
\vec{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}^{\mathsf{T}}
. The virtual localization formula of
[
54
,
23
]
computes the integrals in (
2.28
) as a sum over these fixed points, giving the instanton partition function as a combinatorial expansion in
\mathtt{q}
whose terms are rational functions of the equivariant parameters
(\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon},\vec{m})
. It reads as

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}^{r}(\mathtt{q};\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon},\vec{m})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\mathtt{q}^{k}\ \sum_{\vec{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}^{\mathsf{T}}}\,\frac{\sqrt{e_{\mathsf{T}}}\big(({\sf Ob}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}\big)\ e_{\mathsf{T}}\big((\mathscr{V}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}\otimes{\bm{r}}\big)}{e_{\mathsf{T}}\big(T_{\vec{\sigma}}\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}\big)}\ .

(2.32)

The fixed points of the instanton moduli spaces have a combinatorial significance that we discuss below. The square root Euler class
\sqrt{e_{\mathsf{T}}}\big(({\sf Ob}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}\big)
in (
2.32
) is defined up to a sign which depends explicitly on the orientation of
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}
at the fixed point
\vec{\sigma}
. The formula (
2.32
) assumes that the spaces
T_{\vec{\sigma}}\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
and
({\sf Ob}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}
carry only non-zero weights for the action of the torus
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon}}\,
, so that the equivariant Chow cohomology classes
e_{\mathsf{T}}(T_{\vec{\sigma}}\mathfrak{M}_{r,k})
and
\sqrt{e_{\mathsf{T}}}\big(({\sf Ob}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}\big)
are invertible. We shall elaborate further on these points later on in this section.

The equivariant theory also allows for the definition of the instanton partition function of the cohomological gauge theory without matter
[
19
]
, that is, the “pure” holomorphic
\mathcal{N}_{\textrm{\tiny T}}=2
topological Yang–Mills theory in eight dimensions defined by integrating
1
over
[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}
. This is analogous to the definition of the Nekrasov partition function for the pure
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory in four dimensions
[
31
]
. The field theory is now equivariant with respect to the torus
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon}}\subset\mathsf{T}
, and the localization theorem gives

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}^{r}(\Lambda;\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon}\,)^{\rm pure}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\Lambda^{k}\ \int_{[\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}]_{\mathtt{o}}^{\rm vir}}^{\mathsf{T}_{\vec{a},\vec{\epsilon}}}\,1=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\Lambda^{k}\ \sum_{\vec{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}^{\mathsf{T}}}\,\frac{\sqrt{e_{\mathsf{T}}}\big(({\sf Ob}_{r,k})_{\vec{\sigma}}\big)}{e_{\mathsf{T}}\big(T_{\vec{\sigma}}\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}\big)}\ .

(2.33)

2.4
Noncommutative Field Theory

As we discussed in Section
2.1
, the noncommutative deformation is equivalent to the choice of a complex structure on
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
, which induces a Poisson bivector
\theta=\zeta\,\omega^{-1}
that we wish to quantize, where
\zeta\in\mathbbm{R}_{>0}
and
\omega
is the corresponding Kähler form of
\mathbbm{R}^{8}
.
The quantization map sends the local complex coordinates
z_{a},\bar{z}_{\bar{a}}
of
M=\mathbbm{C}^{4}
to operators with the commutation relations

\displaystyle[z_{a},z_{b}]=0=[\bar{z}_{\bar{a}},\bar{z}_{\bar{b}}]\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad[z_{a},\bar{z}_{\bar{b}}]=\zeta\,\delta_{a\bar{b}}\ ,

(2.34)

for
a,\bar{a},b,\bar{b}\in\underline{\sf 4}
. We write
\mathcal{A}
for the noncommutative algebra generated by these operators over
\mathbbm{C}
. The unique irreducible representation of this algebra is given by the Fock module

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\mathbbm{C}[\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2},\bar{z}_{3},\bar{z}_{4}]|\vec{0}\,\rangle=\bigoplus_{\vec{n}\in\mathbbm{Z}_{\geq 0}^{4}}\,\mathbbm{C}|\vec{n}\,\rangle\ ,

(2.35)

with vacuum vector
|\vec{0}\,\rangle
.

Let
W\simeq\mathbbm{C}^{r}
be the fundamental representation of the colour symmetry
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}
, regarded as a Hermitian vector space.
Using the quantization map, we send all fields of the eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theory to operators acting on the separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{r}:=W\otimes\mathcal{H}
, i.e. to elements of the algebra
{\sf Mat}_{r{\times}r}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathsf{End}_{\mathbbm{C}}(W)\otimes\mathcal{A}
of
r{\times}r
matrices valued in
\mathcal{A}
. This turns the gauge theory into an infinite-dimensional matrix model. The fixed point equations (
2.23
) become operator algebraic equations for the noncommutative fields given by

\displaystyle[Z_{a},Z_{b}]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab\bar{c}\bar{d}}\,\big[Z^{\dagger}_{\bar{c}},Z^{\dagger}_{\bar{d}}\big]\ ,\quad\sum_{a=1}^{4}\,\big[Z_{\bar{a}}^{\dagger},Z_{a}\big]=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad[Z_{a},\varphi]=0\,

(2.36)

for
a,b\in\underline{\sf 4}
, where the operators
Z_{a}:=\frac{1}{2}\,(z_{a}+\mathrm{i}\,A_{\bar{a}})
are called
covariant coordinates
.
The
\varOmega
-deformation leaves unchanged the instanton equations but alters the last equation of (
2.36
) to

\displaystyle[Z_{a},\varphi]=\epsilon_{a}\,Z_{a}\ .

(2.37)

It follows that charge
k
noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(r)
instantons in eight dimensions are described as elements of the algebra
{\sf Mat}_{r{\times}r}(\mathcal{A})
acting on the free
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{A}^{r}:=W\otimes\mathcal{A}
. Through the natural isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}
-modules
\mathcal{E}^{r,k}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{r}
, they can be related to connections
\nabla:\mathcal{E}^{r,k}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{r,k}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}
on the projective modules
\mathcal{E}^{r,k}:=\mathcal{H}^{k}\oplus\mathcal{A}^{r}
over
\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{H}^{k}:=V\otimes\mathcal{H}
for a Hermitian vector space
V\simeq\mathbbm{C}^{k}
and
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}
is the bimodule of one-forms over the algebra
\mathcal{A}
.
This induces a decomposition of the covariant coordinates
Z_{a}\in\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{E}^{r,k})
as

\displaystyle Z_{1}={\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B_{1}&\hat{I}_{1}\\
\hat{I}^{\prime}_{1}&R_{1}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\ ,\quad Z_{2}={\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B_{2}&\hat{I}_{2}^{\prime}\\
\hat{I}_{2}&R_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\ ,\quad Z_{3}={\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B_{3}&\hat{I}_{3}^{\prime}\\
\hat{I}_{3}&R_{3}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad Z_{4}={\small\bigg(\begin{matrix}B_{4}&\hat{I}_{4}^{\prime}\\
\hat{I}_{4}&R_{4}\end{matrix}\bigg)}\normalsize\ ,

(2.38)

where the diagonal blocks consist of linear maps
B_{a}\in\mathsf{End}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V)
and operators
R_{a}\in\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(W\otimes\mathcal{A})
, while the off-diagonal blocks are operators

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{1},\hat{I}_{2}^{\prime},\hat{I}_{3}^{\prime},\hat{I}_{4}^{\prime}\ \in\ \mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(W\otimes\mathcal{A},V)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\hat{I}_{1}^{\prime},\hat{I}_{2},\hat{I}_{3},\hat{I}_{4}\ \in\ \mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(V,W\otimes\mathcal{A})\ .

(2.39)

We can set
\hat{I}_{a}^{\prime}=0
for
a\in\underline{\sf 4}
thanks to the freedom of gauge choice.

Using the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(W\otimes\mathcal{A},V)&\simeq\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}(W,V)\otimes\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}^{r})\end{split}

(2.40)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(V,W\otimes\mathcal{A})&\simeq\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V,W)\otimes\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}^{r})\end{split}

(2.41)

we decompose the operators from (
2.39
) as
\hat{I}_{a}=I_{a}\otimes\psi_{a}
, where
I_{1}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}(W,V)
,
I_{\alpha}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V,W)
for
\alpha\in\{2,3,4\}
and the operators
\psi_{a}\in\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}^{r})\simeq{\sf Mat}_{r{\times}r}(\mathcal{A})
satisfy
\psi_{1}\,\psi_{\alpha}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}^{r}}
. Relabelling
I:=I_{1}
and
J_{\alpha}:=I_{\alpha+1}
, it is then straightforward to see that the first and second equations of (
2.36
) yield the matrix equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}[B_{1},B_{\alpha}]+I\,J_{\alpha}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{1\alpha\bar{\beta}\bar{\gamma}}\,\big[B_{\bar{\beta}}^{\dagger},B_{\bar{\gamma}}^{\dagger}\big]&=0\ ,\\[4.0pt]
[B_{\alpha},B_{\beta}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{1\alpha\bar{\beta}\bar{\gamma}}\,\big(\big[B_{\bar{1}}^{\dagger},B_{\bar{\gamma}}^{\dagger}\big]-J_{\bar{\gamma}}^{\dagger}\,I^{\dagger}\big)&=0\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\sum_{a=1}^{4}\,\big[B_{a},B_{\bar{a}}^{\dagger}\big]+I\,I^{\dagger}-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\,J_{\bar{\alpha}}^{\dagger}\,J_{\alpha}&=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}\ ,\end{split}

(2.42)

for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\{1,2,3\}
, along with other relations which are not relevant here.

We observe that the space of solutions to the system of equations (
2.42
) is independent of the value of
\zeta>0
as a consequence of the scaling symmetry
B_{a}\to\kappa\,B_{a}
,
I\to\kappa\,I
,
J_{\alpha}\to\kappa\,J_{\alpha}
and
\zeta\to\kappa^{2}\,\zeta
for
\kappa\in\mathbbm{R}
. If the
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instanton equations reduce to the Hermitian Yang–Mills equations (i.e.
S_{0}=0
), then the first instanton equation of (
2.36
) would reduce to the holomorphic equations
[Z_{a},Z_{b}]=0
and the non-holomorphic terms in the first two matrix equations of (
2.42
) would drop out. In this instance one can repeat the stability argument of
[
6
]
to infer that, in the equivariant gauge theory of Section
2.3
, one may set
J_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}
. In fact, it is the equations
[B_{a},B_{b}]=0
that arise for the vacua in the string theory setting discussed below, which we shall see are equivalent to the non-holomorphic equations with
J_{\alpha}=0
that retain the information of the original
\mathsf{Spin}(7)
-instantons. With this in mind, we restrict the solutions of (
2.42
) to the closed subvariety defined by the condition

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}=0\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\alpha\in\{1,2,3\}\ .

(2.43)

Altogether we have shown that the noncommutative instanton equations (
2.36
) can be reduced to the ADHM-type equations

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{ab}:=[B_{a},B_{b}]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab\bar{c}\bar{d}}\,\big[B_{\bar{c}}^{\dagger},B_{\bar{d}}^{\dagger}\big]=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}:=\sum_{a=1}^{4}\,\big[B_{a},B_{\bar{a}}^{\dagger}\big]+I\,I^{\dagger}=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}\ .

(2.44)

In Appendix
A
we describe how to explicitly construct an
\mathsf{SU}(4)
-instanton connection
A
from the generalized ADHM data.
For later considerations, it is convenient to encode the ADHM data in a framed representation of the four-loop quiver
\mathsf{L}_{4}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boxed{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}

(2.45)

Physical Interpretation

The ADHM equations (
2.44
) describe supersymmetric bound states of
k
coincident D
1
-branes along
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
with
r
D
9
-branes filling
\mathbbm{R}^{1,9}\simeq\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}\times\mathbbm{C}^{4}
in the low energy limit of type IIB string theory (or the T-dual D
0
–D
8
and D
(-1)
–D
7
systems), with a large constant Kalb–Ramond field preserving
\frac{1}{8}
of the supercharges
[
55
,
39
,
19
,
20
,
56
,
57
,
40
]
. The massless spectrum of D9–D9 strings yields ten-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
Yang–Mills theory with gauge group
\mathsf{U}(r)
, and the noncommutative instanton equations (
2.36
) describe D
1
–D
9
-brane bound states from the perspective of the D
9
-branes. Our choice of vanishing first Chern form in Section
2.2
excludes D
7
–D
9
bound states, while the restriction of vanishing second Chern form, i.e.
S_{0}=0
, also excludes D
5
–D
9
bound states.

From the point of view of the D
1
-branes, the D
9
-branes are heavy and the degrees of freedom supported on them are frozen to their vacuum expectation values. Then the Chan–Paton gauge symmetry becomes a global
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}
colour symmetry on the D
9
-branes.
The parameter
\zeta
is determined by the constant background
B
-field along
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
and it plays the role of the coupling of a Fayet–Iliopoulos term in the low energy
\mathcal{N}=(0,2)
field theory with gauge group
\mathsf{U}(k)
on the D
1
-branes. The arrows
B_{a}
of the ADHM quiver representation (
2.45
) for
a\in\underline{\sf 4}
are the lowest components of chiral superfields on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
that arise from quantizing the D
1
–D
1
strings which generate four complex scalar fields in the adjoint representation of
\mathsf{U}(k)
, while the arrow
I
is the lowest component of a chiral superfield on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
associated to the massless spectrum of D
1
–D
9
open strings in the Neveu–Schwarz sector which give rise to a complex scalar field transforming in the bifundamental representation of
\mathsf{U}(k)\times\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}
.

The ADHM equations (
2.44
) are the equations for the classical Higgs branch of the D1-brane theory
[
39
,
40
]
. Since

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}\big)=\mathrm{Tr}\big(I\,I^{\dagger}\big)=\zeta\,k\ ,

(2.46)

and the term in the middle is non-negative, a solution to
\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}
exists only if
\zeta\geq 0
. If
\zeta=0
then
I=0
, which corresponds to the two-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=(8,8)
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory with gauge group
\mathsf{U}(k)
on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
associated to the massless spectrum of D1–D1 strings alone. It can be obtained as the dimensional reduction of ten-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory with gauge group
\mathsf{U}(k)
.

Thus the interesting case for us is
\zeta>0
; in this case the D1–D9-brane system is tachyonic and unstable
[
13
]
, so it decays to a supersymmetric vacuum via tachyon condensation. The presence of the Fayet–Iliopoulos term with coupling
\zeta
in string theory corresponds to a noncommutative deformation of the
\mathsf{U}(r)
gauge theory on the D9-branes
[
58
,
30
]
, and
this is why one has to work with noncommutative instantons. Thus the situation here is somewhat different from the four-dimensional case: for instantons on
\mathbbm{R}^{4}\simeq\mathbbm{C}^{2}
a similar analysis, now done for a D1–D5-brane system, leads to a theory where
\zeta=0
is a permitted and non-trivial choice, though
\zeta\neq 0
prevents the system from entering the Coulomb branch through the small instanton singularity
[
58
]
.

Finally, as discussed in Section
2.3
, the partition function of the topological field theory is defined by adding
r
fundamental fermions whose functional integration brings down the Euler class of the matter bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\otimes{\bm{r}}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
. In string theory language this is conjecturally equivalent to adding
r
anti-D9-branes to the interacting system of D-branes
[
19
,
20
]
, with the masses
\vec{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})
corresponding to the equivariant Chern roots of the Chan–Paton bundle on the
\overline{\rm D9}
-branes. Similarly to the D9–D9 strings, the degrees of freedom supported on the
\overline{\rm D9}
–
\overline{\rm D9}
strings are frozen to their vacuum expectation values, leaving a global
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}
flavour symmetry on the
\overline{\rm D9}
-branes. Although the D9-branes and anti-D9-branes need not annihilate completely to a supersymmetric vacuum in the background of a
B
-field
[
59
]
, any remaining open string ground states will be invisible to the D1-branes.

Altogether the spectrum of stable states (free from tachyonic modes) in the presence of
r
anti-D9-branes involves reversed GSO projections and changes only the Fermi field content in the
\mathcal{N}=(0,2)
field theory on the D
1
-branes, adding to the ADHM data an antichiral spinor on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,1}
in the bifundamental representation of
\mathsf{U}(k)\times\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}
associated to the D1–
\overline{\rm D9}
-strings from the Ramond sector, whose lowest component we denote by
\bar{I}
. It does not modify the form of the ADHM equations (
2.44
) nor the non-trivial values of the Fayet–Iliopoulos coupling
\zeta
.

Geometrical Interpretation

The moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
of
\mathsf{U}(r)
noncommutative
k
-instantons can be described as the ADHM moduli space, that is, the space of quintuples
(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}
satisfying the ADHM equations (
2.44
) modulo the action of the unitary group
\mathsf{G}^{\textrm{\tiny$V$}}=\mathsf{U}(k)
of the vector space
V
given by

\displaystyle g\cdot(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}=(g\,B_{a}\,g^{-1},g\,I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad g\in\mathsf{U}(k)\ ,

(2.47)

which leaves invariant the ADHM equations (
2.44
). In this parametrization, the vector space
V
descends to
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
as the vector bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}
discussed in Section
2.3
.

Let us
consider the complex moment map equations in (
2.44
), given by
\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{ab}=0
for
1\leq a<b\leq 4
. As noted by
[
19
]
, there is an identity

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}\,\big\|\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{ab}\big\|_{\textrm{\tiny F}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}\,\big\|[B_{a},B_{b}]\big\|_{\textrm{\tiny F}}^{2}\ ,

(2.48)

where
\|-\|_{\textrm{\tiny F}}
is the Frobenius norm on
\mathsf{End}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V)
. From (
2.48
) it follows that the equations
\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{ab}=0
are equivalent to the equations
[B_{a},B_{b}]=0
. Instead, the real moment map equation
\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}
in (
2.44
) for
\zeta>0
is equivalent to a
stability
condition:
There is no proper subspace
S\subset\mathbbm{C}^{k}
such that
B_{a}(S)\subset S
for
a\in\underline{\sf 4}
and
{\rm im}(I)\subset S
. The stability condition is equivalent to the condition that the operators
B_{a}
for
a\in\underline{\sf 4}
acting on
{\rm im}(I)
generate the vector space
V=\mathbbm{C}[B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}]\,I(W)
; it can be interpreted physically as the requirement that all D1-branes are bound to D9-branes.

Writing
\mathfrak{B}^{\rm st}
for the space of stable quintuples, it follows that the instanton moduli space has an explicit holomorphic parametrization as the geometric invariant theory (GIT) quotient

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}=\big\{(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\in\mathfrak{B}^{\rm st}\ \big|\ [B_{a},B_{b}]=0\ ,\ 1\leq a<b\leq 4\big\}\,\big/\,\mathsf{GL}(k,\mathbbm{C})\ .

(2.49)

From this one easily sees that the real virtual dimension of
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
is
(8\,k^{2}+2\,r\,k)-6\,k^{2}-2\,k^{2}=2\,r\,k
.

As a consequence, by results of
[
60
,
61
]
the instanton moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
is isomorphic to the Quot scheme
{\sf Quot}^{k}_{r}(\mathbbm{C}^{4})
of zero-dimensional quotients of the free sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbbm{C}^{4}}^{\oplus r}
with length
k
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}\,\simeq\,{\sf Quot}^{k}_{r}(\mathbbm{C}^{4})\ ,

(2.50)

which parametrizes framed torsion free sheaves
\mathcal{E}
on complex projective space
{\mathbbm{P}}^{4}
of rank
r
and
\mathrm{ch}_{4}(\mathcal{E})=k
. Any such sheaf sits in a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbbm{P}}^{4}}^{\oplus r}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{Z}\longrightarrow 0\ ,

(2.51)

where
\mathcal{S}_{Z}
is a pure torsion sheaf of length
k
supported on a zero-dimensional subscheme
Z\subset{\mathbbm{P}}^{4}
.

Remark 2.52
.

In the rank one case
r=1
, it follows from (
2.50
) that the instanton moduli space is isomorphic to the Hilbert scheme of
k
points on
\mathbbm{C}^{4}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{1,k}\,\simeq\,{\sf Hilb}^{k}(\mathbbm{C}^{4})\ ,

(2.53)

which is parametrized by ideals
J
of codimension
k
in the polynomial ring
\mathbbm{C}[z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}]
. The correspondence follows from defining the
k
-dimensional vector space
V=\mathbbm{C}[z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}]/J
for an ideal
J\in{\sf Hilb}^{k}(\mathbbm{C}^{4})
. We then take
B_{a}\in\mathsf{End}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V)
to be given as multiplication by
z_{a}
mod
J
and
I\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}(\mathbbm{C},V)
as
I(1)=1
mod
J
.

2.5
Quiver Matrix Model

From the perspective of D
9
-branes, the noncommutative field theory of Section
2.4
computes the instanton partition function (
2.28
) for the cohomological field theory of Section
2.2

[
41
]
. Here we shall study the theory from the perspective of the D
1
-branes. Following
[
6
,
7
]
, this uses the generalized ADHM parametrization of the instanton moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{r,k}
to construct a matrix model representation of the integral in (
2.28
), which is based on the fields of the quiver (
2.45
). The construction of the cohomological matrix model proceeds analogously to the construction of the eight-dimensional cohomological gauge theory from
[
47
,
14
]
.

The symmetry group of the quiver matrix model is
\mathsf{U}(k)\times\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}\times\mathsf{SU}(4)
. The gauge and global framing symmetries act on the ADHM variables
(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}
as

\displaystyle(g,h)\cdot(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}=(g\,B_{a}\,g^{-1},g\,I\,h^{-1})_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(g,h)\in\mathsf{U}(k)\times\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}\ .

(2.54)

We work equivariantly with respect to the (complex) maximal torus
\mathsf{T}_{\vec{\epsilon}}
of
\mathsf{SU}(4)
, which acts on the ADHM data as

\displaystyle(t_{a})_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\cdot(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}=(t_{a}^{-1}\,B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad(t_{a})_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}=\big(\mathrm{e}^{\,\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon_{a}}\big)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\in\mathsf{T}_{\vec{\epsilon}}\ .

(2.55)

The BRST multiplets are
(B_{a},\psi_{a})_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}
and
(I,\varrho)
with the BRST transformations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}B_{a}=\psi_{a}\qquad,&\qquad\mathcal{Q}\psi_{a}=[\phi,B_{a}]-\epsilon_{a}\,B_{a}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathcal{Q}I=\varrho\qquad,&\qquad\mathcal{Q}\varrho=\phi\,I-I\,{\bm{a}}\ ,\end{split}

(2.56)

for
a\in\underline{\sf 4}
,
where
\phi
is the generator of
\mathsf{U}(k)
gauge transformations and
{\bm{a}}={\rm diag}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
is a background field which parametrizes an element of the (complex) Cartan subalgebra of
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm col}
.

The matrix fields
(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}
are required to satisfy the seven constraints (
2.44
). Note that the complex moment map equations
\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{ab}=0
, for
a,b\in\underline{\sf 4}
with
a<b
, only contribute three independent constraints, which we choose to be

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}=0\qquad\mbox{with}\quad(\alpha,\beta)\ \in\ \underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}:=\big\{(1,2)\,,\,(1,3)\,,\,(2,3)\big\}\ .

(2.57)

To implement the equations (
2.57
) and
\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}=\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}
in (
2.44
), we add the Fermi multiplets
(\vec{\chi},\vec{H})
, where
\vec{\chi}=(\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta},\chi^{\mathbbm{R}})_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}}
are the antighost fields in
\mathsf{End}_{\mathbbm{C}}(V)
and
\vec{H}=(H_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathbbm{C}},H^{\mathbbm{R}})_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}}
are the auxiliary fields. Their BRST transformations are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}=H^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}\qquad,&\qquad\mathcal{Q}H^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}=[\phi,\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}]-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathcal{Q}\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}=H^{\mathbbm{R}}\qquad,&\qquad\mathcal{Q}H^{\mathbbm{R}}=[\phi,\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}]\ ,\end{split}

(2.58)

for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}
, where generally we abbreviate
\epsilon_{ab\cdots}:=\epsilon_{a}+\epsilon_{b}+\cdots
throughout. Finally, we add the scalar gauge multiplet
(\phi,\bar{\phi},\eta)
to close the algebra of BRST transformations as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\phi=0\ ,\quad\mathcal{Q}\bar{\phi}=\eta\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{Q}\eta=[\bar{\phi},\phi]\ .

(2.59)

We also add matter fields to the theory, associated to the matter bundle
\mathscr{V}_{r,k}\otimes{\bm{r}}
, which transform under a
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}
flavour symmetry. Corresponding to
r
anti-D
9
-branes, in the cohomological matrix model we include the Fermi multiplet
(\bar{I},\bar{\varrho})
of fields in
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathbbm{C}}({\bm{r}},V)
with the BRST transformations

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\bar{I}=\bar{\varrho}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{Q}\bar{\varrho}=\phi\,\bar{I}-\bar{I}\,{\bm{m}}\ ,

(2.60)

where the masses
{\bm{m}}={\rm diag}(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})
parametrize an element of the (complex) Cartan subalgebra of
\mathsf{U}(r)_{\rm fla}
.

The action functional which corresponds to this system of fields and equations is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}S&=\mathcal{Q}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}}\,{\chi}_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathbbm{C}\,\dagger}\,\big(g_{\mathbbm{C}}\,{H}^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}-\mu^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}\big)+\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}\,\big(g_{\mathbbm{R}}\,H^{\mathbbm{R}}-\mu^{\mathbbm{R}}-\zeta\,\mathbbm{1}_{k}\big)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\,\psi_{a}\,\big[\bar{\phi},B^{\dagger}_{a}\big]+I^{\dagger}\,\bar{\phi}\,\varrho+\bar{I}^{\dagger}\,\bar{\phi}\,\bar{\varrho}+\eta\,\big[\phi,\bar{\phi}\,\big]+{\rm c.c.}\Big)\\
&\quad\,+g_{1}\,\mathcal{Q}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}\,\bar{\phi}\,\big)+g_{2}\,\mathcal{Q}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Big(\sum_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}\,\big(B_{a}\,\psi_{a}^{\dagger}-\psi_{a}\,B_{a}^{\dagger}\big)+I\,\varrho^{\dagger}-\varrho\,I^{\dagger}+\bar{I}\,\bar{\varrho}^{\dagger}-\bar{\varrho}\,\bar{I}^{\dagger}\Big)\ ,\end{split}

(2.61)

where
{\rm c.c.}
means complex conjugate and we introduced appropriate coupling constants for convenience. The last two terms have been added by hand to respectively give a nondegenerate mass matrix for
\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}
as well as kinetic terms for
\psi_{a}
,
\varrho
and
\bar{\varrho}
.
After dividing out by the volume of the gauge group, the path integral form of the equivariant integral over the ADHM variables can then be written symbolically as

\displaystyle\int\,\frac{\mathscr{D}[{\rm fields}]}{{\tiny\tt vol}\big(\mathsf{U}(k)\big)}\ \mathrm{e}^{-S}\ .

(2.62)

To evaluate the integral (
2.62
), we follow the technique employed in
[
6
]
.
The first step is to use the
\mathsf{U}(k)
gauge invariance to diagonalize the gauge generator
\phi
. This produces a measure on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
with Vandermonde determinant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k!}\,\prod_{i=1}^{k}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\phi_{i}}{2\pi\,\mathrm{i}}\ \prod^{k}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle i,j=1}}{{\scriptstyle i\neq j}}}\,(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j})\ ,

(2.63)

where
k!
is the order of the Weyl group of
\mathsf{U}(k)
.

By construction of the cohomological theory, the path integral (
2.62
) is independent of the coupling constants
g_{\mathbbm{C}}
,
g_{\mathbbm{R}}
,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
. Taking the limit
g_{1}\rightarrow\infty
, the relevant part of the action functional is
\mathrm{Tr}(H^{\mathbbm{R}}\,\bar{\phi}+\chi^{\mathbbm{R}}\,\eta)
, which shows that the fields
(H^{\mathbbm{R}},\bar{\phi},\chi^{\mathbbm{R}},\eta)
can simply be integrated out and do not contribute non-trivially to the path integral.

Sending
g_{\mathbbm{C}}\rightarrow\infty
, the relevant part of the action functional is

\displaystyle\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Big(H_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathbbm{C}\,{\dagger}}\,H^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}+\chi_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathbbm{C}\,{\dagger}}\,\big([\phi,\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}]-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}\big)\Big)\ .

(2.64)

This shows that the auxiliary fields
H^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}
do not contribute non-trivially. On the other hand, integrating the antighost fields
\chi^{\mathbbm{C}}_{\alpha\beta}
produces

\displaystyle\prod_{i,j=1}^{k}\ \prod_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\underline{\sf 3}^{\perp}}\,(\phi_{i}-\phi_{j}-\epsilon_{\alpha\beta})\ ,

(2.65)

which contributes to the equivariant Euler class of the self-dual obstruction bundle
{\sf Ob}_{r,k}^{-}
, with global orientation corresponding to the choice (
2.57
) and our choice of ordering of antighosts in the Grassmann integration measure.

Finally, for
g_{2}\rightarrow\infty
the contribution of the ADHM fields
(B_{a},I)_{a\in\underline{\sf 4}}
to the path integral (
2.62
) is
